=== PROMPT VARIANT ===

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given an integer sequence of length N: (A_1, A_2, \ldots, A_N). There is also a sequence S, which is initially empty.
For each i = 1, 2, \ldots, N in this order, you perform exactly one of the following two operations:

- Append A_i as an element to the end of S.
- Delete the last element of S. You cannot choose this operation if S is empty.

Print the maximum possible value of the sum of the elements of S after all operations.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
N
A_1 A_2 \ldots A_N

Output

Print the answer.

Constraints


- 1 \leq N \leq 2 \times 10^5
- -10^9 \leq A_i \leq 10^9
- All input values are integers.

Sample Input 1

6
3 -1 -4 5 -9 2

Sample Output 1

8

Starting from the initial state where S is an empty sequence, consider the following operations:

- For i = 1, append A_1 = 3 to the end of S. Now, S = (3).
- For i = 2, append A_2 = -1 to the end of S. Now, S = (3, -1).
- For i = 3, delete the last element of S. Now, S = (3).
- For i = 4, append A_4 = 5 to the end of S. Now, S = (3, 5).
- For i = 5, append A_5 = -9 to the end of S. Now, S = (3, 5, -9).
- For i = 6, delete the last element of S. Now, S = (3, 5).

Here, the sum of the elements of S after all operations is 3 + 5 = 8, which is the maximum possible value.

Sample Input 2

1
-1

Sample Output 2

-1

Note that if S is empty, you must choose to append an element.

Sample Input 3

20
-14 74 -48 38 -51 43 5 37 -39 -29 80 -44 -55 59 17 89 -37 -68 38 -16

Sample Output 3

369

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Let's think if there are any hidden pitfalls: The operation "Append A_i as an element to the end of S" or "Delete the last element of S" exactly one. Our balanced gap construction uses first m indices in gap as append, last m as pop. But if a gap is between survivors f_k and f_{k+1}, after appending f_k, the stack height is k. We then append m elements, stack height k+m. Then pop m times, each pop removes the last appended among the m (since they are above f_k), returning to height k. Then append f_{k+1}. This works regardless of values. For prefix before f1, stack empty; append m, pop m. For suffix after fL, append m, pop m. Thus any F with even gaps valid. If F empty and N even, choose prefix? Actually empty final stack means no survivors; the whole sequence must be balanced. For N even, choose first N/2 appends, last N/2 pops? Wait if all operations: first N/2 append, then N/2 pop, final empty. This is valid if N even. But our gap condition with L=0: entire sequence gap length N must be even. So empty valid for N even. Our DP answer max(0, dp_even) handles.

Need ensure dp_even for N even includes all nonempty valid F ending at even original index. But what about final stack empty and N even but dp_even maybe >0; max with 0. Good. If N even and dp_even negative, empty better. If N even and dp_even positive, choose nonempty. If N even and dp_even = -inf impossible but okay.

What about N odd and final stack cannot be empty; but could choose F with L=0 and N odd? Whole sequence length odd cannot be balanced because balanced sequence length even. So no. Our answer dp_odd. But is it possible to have final stack with last original index odd but L even? No, f_L parity = L mod2, so if last odd, L odd. Since N odd, L odd. Good.

Now, is there any possibility to choose a final stack where an element appended in a gap is popped after next survivor but before end without popping survivor? No, because next survivor is above it; cannot pop below without popping survivor. Since survivor final, impossible. Thus gap balanced necessity.

Let's consider if values of popped elements can somehow affect final sum? No, final sum only remaining elements. Our subsequence sum ignores popped elements. In construction, popped elements in balanced gaps can be any values; they don't contribute. Good.

Now, let's think if there is an alternative DP maybe with "maximum sum of alternating parity subsequence" but need ensure we don't accidentally allow choosing an odd index after empty at any time, which corresponds to starting a new "top-level" survivor after a balanced prefix. Let's attempt to derive DP from another perspective to ensure update order with dp_odd/dp_even not using current index incorrectly. For odd i:

Uses dp_odd from previous odd indices. Since i even, no odd at same i. Fine.

Let's maybe derive an invariant for DP to ensure no off-by-one:
Let `best_odd` be max sum of a valid nonempty subsequence ending at an odd index (1-based) after processing some prefix. Let `best_even` similarly ending at even index. Initially -inf.
For each i from 1 to N:
- If i odd:
   We may start a new subsequence at i: sum A_i.
   We may append i to any subsequence ending at an even index: best_even + A_i.
   Or keep previous best_odd.
   Update best_odd.
- If i even:
   We may append i to any subsequence ending at an odd index: best_odd + A_i.
   Or keep previous best_even.
   Update best_even.
At end:
- If N odd, final stack must have odd length, so last chosen index odd. Answer best_odd.
- If N even, final stack may be empty (0) or nonempty even length, so last chosen index even. Answer max(0, best_even).

Wait, "final stack must have odd length" if N odd. Does last chosen index odd always imply odd length? Now, let's think if there is a scenario where a gap length even but the simple first-half-open/last-half-close pattern would pop a previous survivor if m=0? no. If m>0, after appending m elements, the top is the last appended; popping m times removes exactly those m, not previous survivors. Good.

Now, maybe there is an alternative operation sequence that yields a final stack not satisfying parity? We argued no due final height parity. But let's prove final height parity: Each operation changes stack height by ±1. Starting 0, after N operations, height parity = N mod2. So final stack length parity = N mod2. Our parity condition also implies selected indices count parity = N. But does selected indices count parity = final stack length? Yes final stack length = number of selected indices. So if N even, L even; if N odd, L odd. Good.

But the alternating parity condition f_k ≡ k mod2 is stronger than just L parity. Need prove from LIFO. Let's prove with "gaps balanced" as above. Suppose final stack F. For each gap between consecutive final elements, any operation in that gap that appends an element must be popped before the next final element is appended; otherwise it would remain above the next final element and be in final stack (since it cannot be popped after next final element without popping next final element). Thus in each gap, number of appends equals number of pops, so gap length even. This implies f_{k+1} - f_k -1 even, so f_{k+1} and f_k have opposite parity. Since f1-1 even, f1 odd. Thus f_k parity alternates starting odd. Good. This is clear.

Sufficiency: Given F with f_k parity k, gaps even. We can fill each gap with a balanced sequence: append for first half, pop for second half. This ensures exactly F remains. This is constructive.

Now, one subtlety: In a gap between final elements, "any operation in that gap that appends an element must be popped before the next final element is appended" is true if it is not itself a final element. But what about an element appended in gap that is popped after the next final element? To pop it after next final element, the next final element would have to be popped first because it is above. Since next final element is never popped, impossible. Thus yes. What about an element appended in gap that remains in final stack but is not listed? Contradiction to F being all final elements. Thus all appends in gap are popped within gap. Therefore number of appends in gap equals number of pops in gap. But gap length = appends + pops, so even. Good.

What about prefix before first final element: any append there must be popped before first final element, because first final element will be above it and never popped. So prefix balanced, length even. Suffix after last final element: any append there must be popped by end, so suffix balanced, length even. Good.

Thus necessary.

Now, let's think if there is any issue with "delete last element of S" operation at an index that is not in a gap but maybe a pop operation can be used to remove a previous final element? No, final elements are never popped by definition. So all pop operations occur in gaps and match appends in same gap. Good.

Now, final code:

```python
import sys

def main():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    if not data:
        return
    N = data[0]
    A = [0] + data[1:1+N]
    NEG = -10**30  # sufficiently small? Sum min -2e14, so -10**30 safe.
    best_odd = NEG
    best_even = NEG
    for i in range(1, N+1):
        x = A[i]
        if i & 1:
            # choose x as first or after even
            cand = best_even + x
            if x > cand:
                cand = x
            if cand > best_odd:
                best_odd = cand
        else:
            cand = best_odd + x
            if cand > best_even:
                best_even = cand
    if N & 1:
        print(best_odd)
    else:
        print(max(0, best_even))

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```

```python
import sys

def main():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    N = data[0]
    A = data[1:]
    NEG = -10**30
    odd = NEG
    even = NEG
    for i, x in enumerate(A, start=1):
        if i & 1:
            cand = even + x
            if x > cand:
                cand = x
            if cand > odd:
                odd = cand
        else:
            cand = odd + x
            if cand > even:
                even = cand
    if N & 1:
        print(odd)
    else:
        print(max(0, even))

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```
